Understood. I agree with this structure and will treat it as the
mandatory Odysseus provider integration standard. We will
establish it before integrating Groq.
## 1. API key storage — Steam Deck host
Agreed structure
STEAM DECK — HOST
```
/home/deck/.config/odysseus/
└── providers/
├── groq/
│ └── odysseus-groq.key
├── cloudflare/
│ └── odysseus-cloudflare.key
├── openai/
│ └── odysseus-openai.key
├── gemini/
│ └── odysseus-gemini.key
├── mistral/
│ └── odysseus-mistral.key
├── cerebras/
│ └── odysseus-cerebras.key
└── openrouter/
└── odysseus-openrouter.key
```
Rules:
- API keys remain outside the project and Git repository.
- Permissions restricted to the `deck` user (`600` for key files).
- No keys in YAML configuration, logs, prompts or backups without
encryption.
- Odysseus accesses the keys through their host-mounted paths.
## 2. Provider organization — Odysseus project
ODYSSEUS — `odysseus-box`
```
/home/deck/OdysseusProject/
└── providers/
├── groq/
│ ├── config.yaml
│ └── logs/
│ ├── api.log
│ └── errors.log
├── cloudflare/
│ ├── config.yaml
│ └── logs/
├── openai/
├── gemini/
├── mistral/
├── cerebras/
└── openrouter/
```
All provider binaries, runtimes and executable integrations remain
inside `odysseus-box`.
The folders above organize configurations and logs; we won't install
unnecessary separate software for each provider.
## 3. Mandatory WebUI and logging features
Here is what I propose we establish as acceptance criteria.
| Requirement | Expected behaviour |
| -------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------
---- |
| Provider connection | Visible status: Online / Offline / Unknown
|
| API authentication | Clearly distinguish invalid key from
connection failure |
| Rate limits | Show quota or rate-limit errors |
| API errors | Understandable message in WebUI
|
| Detailed diagnostics | Full sanitized technical details in logs
|
| Request tracking | Timestamp, provider, model, request ID
|
| Performance | Response time and token usage where
available |
| Provider selection | Clearly identify the active provider and model
|
| Debugging | Separate logs for each provider
|
| Secret protection | Never expose API keys in logs or UI
|
I would also like a Test Connection action for each provider.
Suggested status states:
ONLINE
API successfully tested
DEGRADED
Rate limits or intermittent failures
OFFLINE
Connection unavailable
AUTH ERROR
Invalid or rejected credentials
NOT TESTED
Not yet verified
## 4. Odysseus WebUI customization
Yes, customization is something we should investigate.
I propose three levels:
1. Settings configuration: use existing options wherever possible.
2. Custom dashboard/menu: add provider status, diagnostics and
useful shortcuts.
3. Custom HTML/CSS/JS: only if necessary, preferably through
isolated files rather than modifying core application logic.
We must first inspect how the WebUI is structured. I will not
promise that arbitrary HTML changes are safe until we understand
its frontend architecture.
Any customization should be reversible, backed up and tested after
application updates.
## 5. One additional rule: provider diagnostics must be useful
For every API request, I want enough information to reconstruct a
failure without exposing credentials:
```
Timestamp:
Provider:
Model:
Request ID:
Connection status:
HTTP status:
Response time:
Token usage:
Error category:
Error message:
```
We should also distinguish between:
- Provider unavailable.
- Invalid API key.
- Quota exhausted.
- Unsupported model.
- Malformed request.
- Odysseus internal error.
That distinction will save us considerable debugging time.
My recommendation: before integrating the first provider, we
should inspect Odysseus' existing provider architecture, logging
system and frontend customization options. Then establish this
common structure once, rather than creating seven different
implementations.
This becomes our standard for Groq, Cloudflare, OpenAI, Gemini,
Mistral, Cerebras and OpenRouter.
# Odysseus customization and provider integration plan
I inspected the official repository, including its frontend
organization, application entry point, configuration documentation
and contributor guidance.
My recommendation is clear:
Build the provider infrastructure as one common foundation, but
integrate and validate providers one at a time. Do not customize
the main HTML before that foundation is working.
There is an important distinction between preparing the
architecture for seven providers and activating seven providers
simultaneously.
## 1. What I found in the frontend
Our installed version is on the curated `main` branch, not the fastermoving `dev` branch. The project documentation explicitly
distinguishes the two. We should stay on `main` unless we have a
specific reason to upgrade.

Customization is possible

### My proposed customization method
Keep the original interface intact
Do not rewrite index.html or app.js wholesale.
Create isolated custom components

```
├── css/

```
Connect through a minimal integration point
Add a provider dashboard module or modal using the existing
frontend architecture, after identifying the correct integration point.
The custom dashboard could eventually display:
- Provider connection status.
- Selected model.
- Remaining quota, if the provider exposes it.
- Last successful request.
- Last error.
- API diagnostics.
We must preserve the application's authentication and Content
Security Policy protections. The existing backend includes security
middleware and nonce-based script handling.

I would avoid editing the original HTML until we have a tested

## 2. Should we integrate all seven providers at once?
No. But we should prepare the common architecture for all seven at
once.
This is the important distinction:
## Recommended architecture
Odysseus Provider Manager
Common configuration, API handling, health checks and logging
Groq
Dedicated config + logs
Cloudflare
Dedicated config + logs
OpenAI
Dedicated config + logs
Gemini
Dedicated config + logs
Mistral
Dedicated config + logs
Cerebras
Dedicated config + logs
OpenRouter
Dedicated config + logs
One shared architecture, seven independent provider
configurations.
We should not build seven different API implementations if a
shared provider interface can handle them.
Each provider will have:
- Its own API key.
- Its own configuration.
- Its own logs.
- Its own connection test.
- Its own health status.
But the common infrastructure handles authentication, request
tracking, error classification and diagnostics.
## 3. Recommended implementation sequence
1. Build the common foundation
- Create provider directory structure.
- Establish host-to-container API key access.
- Define configuration and logging standards.
- Define provider health/status API.
- Keep secrets out of logs.
2. Integrate Groq only
Test valid key, invalid key, unavailable endpoint, unsupported
model, rate limit and successful response.
3. Build the custom provider dashboard
Now that we have a working provider, we can build the UI against
real connection and diagnostic data rather than hypothetical data.
4. Integrate Cloudflare
Reuse the same architecture and test it independently.
5. Add the remaining providers one by one
OpenAI → Gemini → Mistral → Cerebras → OpenRouter.
6. Perform a complete integration test
Check provider switching, connection status, error handling,
logging, secret protection and persistence across restarts.
## 4. Important distinction about provider connections
We can configure all seven providers simultaneously, each with its
own credentials. That does not mean we should send requests to all
seven simultaneously.
Your intended arrangement is:
- Seven providers registered.
- Seven independent health states.
- One manually selected provider for each conversation.
- No automatic fallback.
- No automatic paid usage.
This is entirely consistent with the architecture we are designing.

Recommended plan

| Key management | Build once |
| Logging and diagnostics | Build once |
| Provider status API | Build once |
| Groq integration | First |
| Custom provider dashboard | After Groq validation |
ChatGPT said:
| Remaining six integrations | One at a time |
| Core HTML rewrite | Avoid |
| Git branch | Stay on `main` |
One additional precaution: our earlier Email MCP change is a local
source modification. We must preserve it when making backups or
future updates. We should never blindly overwrite the installed
project with a fresh upstream copy.
My conclusion: the repository gives us a workable foundation for a
carefully isolated custom dashboard. We should not modify the
main interface now. First build the common provider infrastructure,
prove it with Groq, and then give Odysseus its dedicated provider
control panel.
That is the approach I recommend. No installation changes are
needed for this planning step.

Odysseus project
How Odysseus Distrobox/Podman is implemented
The Steam Deck host provides the Distrobox environment. odysseus-box is the Ubuntu 24.04
container used for Odysseus' Python runtime and application dependencies.
ChromaDB is a separate Podman container named odysseus-chromadb, accessed locally
through port 8100. It is not the same container as odysseus-box.
Odysseus startup and shutdown are managed by dedicated scripts, rather than an instruction to
start everything automatically at host boot.
Important ownership safeguard:
/home/deck/OdysseusProject/decktools/runtime/chromadb.started-by-odysseus
The marker indicates that Odysseus started ChromaDB. Existing shutdown logic must not stop
ChromaDB if the marker is absent. ChromaDB was observed running without this ownership
marker before the latest startup; the startup message confirmed that ownership remained
unchanged.
Inside the container, /usr/bin/distrobox-host-exec is available for invoking host commands
when necessary. Host-level Distrobox and Podman management should otherwise be performed
on the host.
